Chapter 1: Village Tides

The fishing nets weighed Lyra’s arms down as she hauled them across the weathered dock. Salt-crusted ropes bit into her palms, but she’d long stopped noticing such things. What she did notice was the horizon—that perfect line where sea met sky, promising something beyond Stillwater’s predictable rhythms. There must be more than this. More than mending nets and gutting fish until my hands are permanently stained with scales.

Gulls circled overhead, their raucous cries as familiar as her own thoughts. The morning sun cast long shadows across Stillwater’s jumbled collection of wooden buildings, their thatched roofs silver with dew. Lyra paused, adjusting her grip on the nets. From this vantage point, she could see the entire village—the communal ovens already sending plumes of smoke into the pale blue sky, the boats gently rocking against their moorings, and the villagers beginning their day with the same motions they’d made yesterday and would make tomorrow.

“Lyra! Stop daydreaming and bring those nets!” Her father’s voice carried across the dock, weathered and rough like everything else in Stillwater.

She quickened her pace, the worn planks creaking beneath her bare feet. Her father, Thom Nightwind, stood knee-deep in water beside his boat, his face already reddened by the early morning sun and salt spray. He didn’t look up as she approached, focused on securing a loose plank.

“Sorry,” she murmured, though she knew he barely registered her presence anymore. As the middle of five children, Lyra had mastered the art of fading into the background. Her older brothers had already apprenticed to fishermen in neighboring villages, while her younger sisters still commanded attention with their childish needs and demands. Lyra existed in the space between—useful but unremarkable.

She handed over the nets and stepped back, waiting for further instructions that didn’t come. Her father had already moved on to the next task, his mind on the day’s catch rather than his middle daughter. Lyra lingered a moment longer before turning away.

“I’m going to help Merina this morning,” she called over her shoulder. Her father grunted in acknowledgment, which was as close to permission as she was likely to get.

The path to the oracle’s hut wound away from the docks, climbing slightly up the gentle slope that protected Stillwater from the worst of the sea storms. Wild herbs grew alongside it—rosemary and sage that her mother, Elinna, harvested for her healing tinctures. Lyra plucked a sprig of lavender as she walked, rolling it between her fingers and inhaling the sharp, clean scent.

Merina’s hut stood apart from the others, its weathered walls hung with wind chimes fashioned from shells and driftwood. The delicate sounds created a boundary between the ordinary world and the oracle’s domain. Lyra hesitated at the threshold, as she always did. No matter how many times she visited, a small flutter of anticipation stirred in her chest.

“Come in, child. The water is already boiling.” Merina’s voice drifted from within before Lyra could announce herself. This no longer surprised her—the village oracle always seemed to know who approached her door before they knocked.

Lyra ducked inside, blinking as her eyes adjusted to the dimmer light. The interior smelled of herbs, sea salt, and beeswax candles. Merina sat cross-legged on a woven mat, her silver hair loose around her shoulders. Though the exact number of her years remained a mystery, her face was a map of fine wrinkles, particularly around her eyes—evidence of a life spent squinting at both sun and stars.

“Good morning,” Lyra said, settling onto the mat across from her. “Father’s taken the boat out early today. They’re hoping for a better catch than yesterday.”

Merina nodded, her gaze appearing both focused on Lyra and somehow distant. “The tide is more favorable today. The moon pulled strongly last night.” She poured steaming water into two clay cups, dropping pinches of dried leaves into each. “But you didn’t come to discuss fishing prospects.”

It wasn’t a question. Lyra cupped her hands around the warm tea, inhaling the fragrant steam. “I had the dream again.”

Merina’s eyes sharpened, her attention fully present now. “The same one? Water and fire?”

Lyra nodded, a chill running through her despite the warm tea in her hands. “I was drowning, but my skin burned as if I’d been thrown into a forge. And there was… a voice.” She hesitated, struggling to capture the fading fragments of the dream. “Not words exactly, but a presence. Something ancient and angry.”

The oracle was silent for a long moment, studying Lyra’s face as if reading a complicated text. Finally, she sighed. “Dreams of opposing elements—water and fire, together—they speak of transformation, child. Great change.”

“But what does it mean?” Lyra asked, leaning forward. This conversation had repeated itself over the past several weeks, but Merina’s interpretations remained frustratingly vague.

The old woman smiled, the expression creating new constellations of wrinkles around her eyes. “It means your life will not always be mending nets and sorting herbs for me, though you do both admirably.”

Lyra tried to hide her disappointment. She’d hoped for something more concrete—a direction, a sign of what path to take. At nineteen, she should have already chosen between joining the fishing fleet or training more formally as a healer under her mother. Instead, she drifted between roles, assisting where needed but committing to nothing.

“Here.” Merina reached for a small wooden chest beside her. From it, she withdrew a smooth stone pendant on a leather cord. “The dreams grow stronger. You should wear this.”

Lyra accepted the pendant. It was cool to the touch, deep blue with veins of silver running through it. “What is it?”

“Lapis lazuli. A stone of truth and protection.” Merina’s fingers, gnarled with age but still nimble, closed Lyra’s hand around it. “It will help you remain anchored when the currents try to sweep you away.”

Lyra slipped the cord over her head, feeling the weight of the stone settle against her chest. “Thank you.” Though she wasn’t certain she believed in the stone’s power, Merina’s gifts were rare and always thoughtfully chosen.

The morning passed in comfortable routine. Lyra ground herbs for Merina’s remedies, sorted through the oracle’s collection of shells and stones, and transcribed the old woman’s observations about tides and lunar phases into the village records. It was peaceful work, requiring just enough attention to quiet her restless thoughts without fully engaging her mind.

Shortly after midday, their routine was interrupted by a commotion from the village center. Voices raised in excitement carried to the oracle’s hut, along with the unfamiliar clanking of metal and stamping of hooves.

“Ah,” Merina said, looking up from the dried seaweed she was sorting. “Merrick’s early this season.”

“The merchant?” Lyra set aside her mortar and pestle. Merrick’s visits were infrequent but always brought welcome variety to village life—new goods, news from distant ports, and occasionally travelers with strange accents and stranger stories.

Merina nodded. “Go. Your curiosity is making your fingers clumsy anyway.” There was no reproach in her tone, only fond understanding.

Lyra didn’t need to be told twice. She rinsed her herb-stained hands in a basin of water and hurried outside, following the sounds of excitement to the village square.

Merrick’s wagon was already surrounded by villagers, children darting between adults for a better view of the merchant’s wares. The man himself stood beside his wagon, gesturing expansively as he spoke with the village elder. Tall and broad-shouldered, with a beard that contained more gray than when Lyra had last seen him, Merrick cut an imposing figure in his traveled-stained cloak.

Lyra joined the crowd, standing on tiptoe to see over shoulders. The wagon was laden with fabrics in colors rarely seen in Stillwater, tools of finer make than their local blacksmith could produce, and small luxuries like sugar and spices. But it was the conversation, not the goods, that caught her attention.

“…third village razed in as many months,” Merrick was saying, his voice grave. “Blackwood’s fleet grows bolder with each success.”

The elder, a stooped man named Gareth, shook his head. “Surely the coastal patrol vessels offer some protection?”

Merrick laughed without humor. “The kingdoms squabble among themselves while pirates rule the sea lanes. Blackwood outmaneuvers the patrol ships at every turn.”

“Blackwood?” Lyra found herself speaking before she could think better of it. “Captain Raven Blackwood?”

Heads turned, and Lyra immediately regretted drawing attention to herself. But Merrick regarded her with interest.

“You know of the Pirate Queen, girl?” he asked.

Lyra felt heat rise to her cheeks. “Only stories. They say her ship is faster than any in the kingdoms, and that she never leaves survivors.”

“Not stories enough, it seems.” Merrick’s expression darkened. “The rumors of her cruelty fall short of the truth. But there’s something new driving her raids.” He lowered his voice, though in the crowded square, many could still hear. “They say she seeks artifacts of power—ancient weapons and relics. She questions survivors before…” He glanced at the children in the crowd and left the sentence unfinished.

A strange chill washed over Lyra, reminiscent of her dreams—that sensation of burning while drowning. Something about Merrick’s words resonated with a half-forgotten memory. Hadn’t Merina once mentioned a legend about a powerful weapon hidden near Stillwater generations ago?

“What sort of artifacts?” she asked, ignoring the disapproving glance from Elder Gareth at her continued interruption.

Merrick shrugged. “Old magic. The kind wise folk leave buried.” He turned back to his wagon, clearly considering the topic closed. “Now, who’s interested in finest thread from the Eastern Kingdoms? Or perhaps some medicinal herbs impossible to grow in your salt-laden soil?”

The crowd’s attention shifted back to commerce, but Lyra stood rooted in place, her hand unconsciously rising to touch the lapis lazuli pendant at her throat. The burning water of her dreams suddenly seemed less abstract, more ominous.

As if sensing her thoughts, a cool breeze swept in from the sea, carrying the tang of salt and the distant cry of gulls. Lyra turned to look back toward the horizon, that endless line between known and unknown. For the first time, the sight brought more anxiety than longing.

There must be more than this, she had thought that morning. Now, watching the bustling activity around Merrick’s wagon, she wondered if “more” might come in forms she wasn’t prepared to face.

Overhead, storm clouds gathered on the distant horizon, though the sky above Stillwater remained clear. Like everything else about this day, it felt like a warning—or perhaps a promise—of changes to come.

Chapter 2: Blood and Blade

The storm arrived three days after Merrick’s warning.

Lyra woke to the distant boom of thunder, different from the familiar rhythm of waves against Stillwater’s shore. She sat up in her narrow bed, the lapis lazuli pendant cool against her skin despite the humid night air. Outside her window, the moon struggled against encroaching clouds, casting fitful shadows across the floor.

Something’s wrong. The thought came unbidden, accompanied by a prickling sensation at the base of her neck.

She slipped from beneath her thin blanket and padded to the window. The village lay quiet below, thatched roofs silvered by moonlight, but beyond—out where sea met sky—pinpricks of light dotted the darkness. Not stars. Not fishing lanterns either; the fleet wouldn’t venture out with a storm approaching.

Her throat tightened. Ships. Too many ships.

A sharp rap at the front door startled her. Voices murmured below—her father’s sleep-rough grunt, then another voice, urgent and breathless. Lyra pulled a shawl around her shoulders and crept to the top of the narrow stairs.

“…Blackwood’s fleet,” the village watchman was saying, his words sending ice through Lyra’s veins. “Six ships at least, approaching fast. Elder Gareth says to gather only what you can carry.”

“And go where?” her father demanded. “There’s nowhere to hide an entire village.”

“The caves beyond the northern point. Merina says—”

“Merina says, Merina says,” her father interrupted. “I trust the sea over an old woman’s feelings.”

Lyra’s mother appeared from the bedroom, already dressed. “Thom,” she said quietly. “Wake the girls.”

Something in her mother’s tone silenced further argument. Her father nodded tersely, and the watchman hurried away to the next house.

Events blurred into frantic motion. Her younger sisters, bleary-eyed and confused, were bundled into warm clothes. Her mother packed a small bag with medicines and dried foods. All the while, Lyra’s hands moved automatically, helping, packing, but her mind remained fixed on the lights approaching from the sea.

“Lyra.” Her mother’s voice broke through her daze. “Go to Merina. She’ll need help with the village records.”

The order surprised her. “But shouldn’t we stay together?”

Her mother’s face softened momentarily. “The old woman has been good to you. And she’ll need strong arms to carry her scrolls.” She pressed something into Lyra’s palm—a small knife in a leather sheath. “Go quickly.”

Outside, the village was awakening to fear. Lanterns bobbed as families emerged from their homes, children clutching prized possessions, parents carrying hastily packed bundles. The orderly evacuation Lyra had expected fragmented as the first distant crack of cannon fire echoed across the water.

Panic rippled through the gathering. Some fled immediately toward the northern path, while others rushed to the docks, determined to launch boats. Lyra pushed against the current of bodies, making for Merina’s isolated hut.

The wind had risen, carrying the scent of rain and something sharper—gunpowder. Another cannon blast, closer now. Lyra broke into a run, her bare feet slipping on the dewy path. The wind chimes outside Merina’s hut jangled frenetically, their usual musical tones transformed into frantic warnings.

“Merina!” she called, pushing through the doorway without her customary pause.

The old woman knelt before a small chest, placing scrolls and bound records inside. She looked up, unsurprised by Lyra’s appearance. “Good. You’re here. Take this.” She thrust the chest toward Lyra.

“We need to go,” Lyra urged, taking the surprisingly heavy container. “The pirates—”

“Are already here,” Merina finished. Her calm was unnerving. “There’s no time to reach the caves. We must hide these records.”

“But—”

The old woman’s hands gripped Lyra’s shoulders with unexpected strength. “Listen carefully, child. The Stillwater records contain information Captain Blackwood must not find. Take them to the hollow oak at the edge of the marsh. Hide them and yourself there.”

“What about you?”

Merina’s smile was serene, mismatched against the growing chaos outside. “My path ends here, as I’ve seen for many nights now.”

“No!” The denial burst from Lyra, sudden and fierce. “Come with me. Please.”

A scream cut through the night, followed by the unmistakable crack of splintering wood. The first pirates had reached the village.

“Go,” Merina commanded, pressing something else into Lyra’s hands—a small leather pouch. “When you need answers, look to water and flame together.”

Before Lyra could protest further, the old woman pushed her toward the back of the hut, where a small door led out to the herb garden. “Remember your dreams, Lyra Nightwind. They weren’t warnings—they were preparation.”

The sky beyond had transformed, orange flames now visible in the village below. The air filled with shouts, screams, and the thunder of approaching boots. Lyra clutched the chest to her body, paralyzed between flight and loyalty.

“GO!” Merina’s command broke her stillness.

Heart pounding in her ears, Lyra ran. Away from the hut, away from the village, toward the dark silhouette of the marsh oak. Behind her, wood cracked and men shouted. She didn’t look back—couldn’t look back—even as Merina’s wind chimes gave one final, discordant jangle before falling silent.

The marsh oak loomed ahead, ancient and gnarled. Its massive trunk, hollowed by lightning strike years before, created a natural hiding place. Lyra squeezed through the opening, scraping her shoulders, and set the chest down in the darkness. She pressed herself as far back as possible, drawing her knees to her chest.

Through the narrow opening, Stillwater burned.

Orange flames leapt from thatched roofs, casting monstrous shadows against the night. Dark figures moved through the village—some running, some pursuing. The screams had resolved into individual voices she recognized. Elder Gareth bellowing orders. The blacksmith’s daughter calling for her father. A child wailing for its mother.

I should be there. I should be helping. The thought twisted in her gut like a knife.

But her body refused to move, frozen by a fear more primal than shame. She watched, silent and trembling, as pirates dragged villagers from hiding places. Through gaps in the trees, she could see figures being herded toward the docks like cattle.

A larger silhouette appeared at the edge of the village—a woman in a long coat, her hair elaborately braided. Even from this distance, her commanding presence was unmistakable. Captain Blackwood had arrived personally to oversee the raid.

The pirate captain surveyed the burning village with casual interest, occasionally giving orders to those around her. When Elder Gareth was brought before her, struggling between two burly pirates, she questioned him briefly before making a dismissive gesture. The elder was added to the group of captives rather than cut down. Blackwood wanted prisoners, not just destruction.

“Please be safe,” Lyra whispered, thinking of her parents and sisters. Guilt churned inside her. She should have stayed with them. She should have insisted Merina come with her. She should have—

Movement near the oracle’s hut caught her attention. Flames now engulfed the small structure, but in their orange light, Lyra could see Merina being escorted—no, dragged—toward Captain Blackwood. Unlike the other villagers, the old woman walked with dignity despite rough handling.

Blackwood stepped forward to meet the oracle, saying something Lyra couldn’t hear. Merina’s response seemed to anger the captain. She gestured sharply, and one of her officers brought forward a scroll—a map or drawing of some kind. Again, Merina spoke, her face calm even as Blackwood’s expression darkened with rage.

What happened next burned itself into Lyra’s memory forever.

Captain Blackwood drew her sword—a fluid, practiced motion—and thrust it through Merina’s chest in a single movement. The old woman’s body jerked, then crumpled to the ground. The captain wiped her blade clean on the oracle’s clothing before turning away, already issuing new orders to her crew.

Lyra bit into her fist to keep from screaming. The metallic taste of blood filled her mouth as her teeth broke skin. Tears streamed down her face, but she didn’t dare make a sound. Not when pirates were still searching the area, torches illuminating their hunt for more villagers.

One such torch came terrifyingly close to the marsh oak. Lyra pressed further back into the hollow, barely breathing as boots crunched on fallen leaves just outside. The torch’s light flickered through the opening, sending shadows dancing across her hiding place.

“Nothing here but an old tree,” a gruff voice said.

“Keep looking,” another responded. “Captain wants them all.”

The torch moved away, and Lyra released her breath in a silent, shuddering exhale. Hours passed this way—crouched in darkness, listening to the destruction of everything she had ever known. The sounds gradually shifted from violence to organized activity. Commands being issued. Supplies being loaded. The creak of wood as ships prepared to depart.

Dawn’s gray light filtered through the opening when Lyra finally dared to move. Her limbs had stiffened painfully, and every muscle protested as she edged toward the hollow’s entrance. The village below lay in ruins. Smoke curled from collapsed buildings. The docks, once crowded with fishing boats, stood empty save for splintered remains.

Most of the pirate ships had departed, their sails now distant smudges on the horizon. One vessel remained, sleek and menacing with crimson sails—the flagship. Figures moved along the shore, gathering final supplies and stragglers.

Lyra knew she should stay hidden. Wait until they were truly gone. But a desperate need to know her family’s fate pushed her forward. Clutching Merina’s pouch and her mother’s knife, she left the chest hidden in the hollow and began a cautious descent toward what remained of Stillwater.

The village was unrecognizable. Charred timbers had replaced familiar homes. The communal ovens lay shattered. Body shapes beneath hastily placed covers marked those who had resisted too fiercely or simply been unlucky. Lyra moved from body to body, dreading what she might find yet unable to stop searching.

She had nearly reached the docks when a hand clamped over her mouth from behind. She tried to scream, to bite, to use the knife still clutched in her fist, but her attacker was too strong.

“Quiet, girl,” a man’s voice hissed in her ear. “Unless you want to join the dead.”

The pirate’s breath was hot against her neck, smelling of rum and rotted teeth. He dragged her toward the waiting ship, where the last boats were being loaded with captives. Through tear-blurred eyes, Lyra saw familiar faces among the prisoners—the blacksmith’s family, several fishermen, children from neighboring houses.

No sign of her parents or sisters.

“Got another one, Captain!” her captor called proudly as they approached the loading area. “Hiding up near the woods!”

Captain Blackwood turned, and Lyra found herself face-to-face with the woman who had destroyed her world. The pirate queen was tall and lean, her black hair intricately braided with gold threads that caught the morning light. A distinctive scar ran from her left temple to jaw. Her crimson coat, edged with gold braid, hung open to reveal practical leather armor beneath.

But it was her eyes that held Lyra captive—cold, calculating, and utterly without mercy.

“Where did you find this one?” Blackwood asked, her voice surprisingly melodic for someone who commanded such violence.

“Skulking near the ruins, Captain.”

Blackwood’s gaze swept over Lyra, assessing. “Search her.”

Rough hands emptied her pockets, confiscated her mother’s knife, and tore Merina’s pouch from her grasp. Lyra barely registered these violations; she was lost in the growing realization that her entire world had ended overnight.

The pirate handed Merina’s pouch to Blackwood, who loosened the drawstring and emptied the contents into her palm. A small object fell out—a carved stone that glinted blue in the morning light. Lyra recognized it as one of the oracle’s treasured divination stones.

Something flickered in Blackwood’s expression—recognition, perhaps even triumph. She closed her fingers around the stone and addressed Lyra directly for the first time.

“You were with the oracle,” she stated rather than asked. “What did she tell you about the artifact?”

Lyra’s mind raced. Artifact? What artifact? She thought of Merina’s final words: When you need answers, look to water and flame together.

“I don’t know what you mean,” she managed, her voice sounding strange to her own ears.

Blackwood studied her for a long moment, then smiled thinly. “Take her with the others. She may prove useful later.”

Lyra was half-dragged, half-carried to the waiting boat. As they shoved her down beside other captives, she caught a last glimpse of Stillwater—smoking ruins where her life had once been. Merina’s body lay where she had fallen, now just one among many shrouded forms scattered throughout the village.

The boat pushed off from shore, its wake spreading ripples across the blood-tinged water. No one spoke. Most captives stared blankly, shock rendering them mute. A child whimpered until its mother pulled it close, murmuring empty reassurances.

Above them loomed the pirate flagship, the Tempest, its crimson sails catching the morning breeze. Lyra felt a strange detachment as she was hauled aboard, her body responding automatically while her mind retreated. Someone bound her wrists with rough rope. Someone else shoved her below decks with the other prisoners. Words were spoken—threats, instructions—but they washed over her without meaning.

As the ship moved away from the shore, Lyra sat in the cramped darkness of the hold, surrounded by the remnants of her village. The numbness that had protected her began to crack, allowing grief to seep through. She bit her lip again to keep from making a sound, tasting fresh blood.

The rocking motion of the ship seemed to mock the stability she had taken for granted her whole life. Everything familiar—gone. Everyone she loved—gone or captured.

And she, who had dreamed of seeing beyond Stillwater’s horizon, was now being carried into that unknown—not as an adventurer but as a prisoner. The bitter irony was not lost on her, even through the haze of shock.

The Tempest cut through the waves, bearing Lyra and the other survivors away from the smoking ruins of their former lives. Behind them, the rising sun painted the sky in shades of blood and fire, a final echo of the night’s destruction.

Somewhere deep in her mind, water and flame mingled once again. Not in dreams this time, but in the harsh reality of what her life had become.

Chapter 3: The Awakening

Time lost meaning in the bowels of the Tempest.

Lyra had tried to count days by the changing of the guards, by the meager meals shoved through the hatch, by the subtle shifts in the ship’s movement. But the rhythm of captivity soon blurred into an endless present—dark, cramped, and reeking of unwashed bodies and fear.

The hold housed thirty-two survivors from Stillwater. Lyra knew because she’d counted them repeatedly, a pointless ritual that nonetheless anchored her to sanity. Some faces were intimately familiar—the blacksmith’s daughter, Nessa, who wept silently each night; old Farro, who had taught Lyra to tie fishing knots when she was small; the Wellen twins, barely ten years old, their eyes enormous in gaunt faces. Others were villagers she’d passed daily but barely spoken to, now bound together in this floating prison.

No sign of her family. This thought returned like the tide, regular and relentless. Lyra clung to the possibility they had escaped to the caves, though hope felt increasingly like self-deception.

A sudden change in the ship’s motion jolted her from half-sleep. The familiar rolling sway transitioned to sharper pitching, accompanied by increased activity overhead. Boots thumped across the deck. Orders were shouted. The Tempest was changing course.

“Storm coming,” Farro murmured beside her, his sea-wisdom undiminished by captivity. “Big one. Feel how she’s battening down?”

Before Lyra could respond, the hatch above creaked open, flooding the dim space with harsh daylight. Silhouetted against the glare stood one of Blackwood’s officers—a square-shouldered woman with a permanently twisted mouth.

“You.” She pointed directly at Lyra. “Captain wants workers. Up.”

Fear and curiosity warred within her as she rose on unsteady legs. Five days in the cramped hold had weakened muscles once tough from village labor. The officer tossed down a rope ladder, and Lyra climbed awkwardly, her bound wrists making each movement a challenge.

The brightness above deck momentarily blinded her. She blinked rapidly, raising her hands to shield against the assault of sunlight. The sky stretched endlessly, a shade of blue that seemed obscene in its beauty after days in darkness. Lyra drank in the sight, filling her lungs with salt air that tasted of freedom even as the guard yanked her roughly forward.

The same process repeated at several other hatches until ten captives stood blinking on the main deck. Pirates moved purposefully around them, securing rigging and stowing loose items. In the distance, a darkness smudged the horizon—the approaching storm Farro had predicted.

“Listen carefully,” the officer announced, pacing before them. “You’re being given the privilege of working rather than rotting below. The storm approaches, and valuables need securing. Do exactly as instructed, and you’ll be rewarded with fresh air and proper food.” Her gaze hardened. “Disobey, attempt escape, or damage anything, and you’ll wish for the simplicity of drowning.”

The captives were divided into pairs, each assigned to different areas of the ship. Lyra found herself partnered with an older Stillwater woman named Marla, whose talent for weaving fishing nets had once been renowned along the coast. Together, they were directed belowdecks again, but to an area far different from the prisoner hold.

“Treasure room needs securing,” their guard explained, unlocking an ornate door reinforced with metal bands. “Pack everything in those crates and lock them down with the brackets on the floor. Nothing breaks, or you’ll replace it with your skin.”

The door swung open to reveal a chamber that might have been transplanted from a royal palace. Lyra couldn’t suppress a small gasp. After days of squalor, the opulence was shocking—gleaming metals, rich fabrics, objects of clearly foreign design arranged on shelves and displayed in glass cases.

“Get to work,” the guard ordered, shoving them inside. “I’ll return when the bell sounds third watch.”

The door locked behind him, leaving Lyra and Marla alone amidst Blackwood’s plunder.

“We should start with the smaller items,” Marla suggested practically, moving toward a display of jeweled daggers. “These will break first in rough seas.”

Lyra nodded, still absorbing the room’s contents. Evidence of multiple raids filled the space—ceremonial masks from the Southern Isles, navigation instruments of Eastern design, books bound in materials she couldn’t identify. Each object represented lives disrupted, possibly ended, by Blackwood’s relentless ambition.

They worked systematically, careful despite their contempt for the pirate queen. Wooden crates lined the walls, each designed to secure specific treasures against the ship’s movement. Lyra found herself cradling delicate artifacts worth more than her entire village, acutely aware that their safety now depended on properly protecting their captor’s prizes.

“What do you suppose this is?” Marla asked, indicating a crystal sphere that seemed to contain swirling mist.

“I don’t know,” Lyra admitted, “but it feels wrong to touch it.”

As they moved deeper into the room, the nature of the collection shifted. The glittering wealth of earlier displays gave way to objects of less obvious value but somehow greater presence—items chosen not for their material worth but for something else entirely.

“These are magical,” Marla whispered, her voice tight with unease. “My grandmother spoke of such things. We shouldn’t linger here.”

But they had no choice. The crates needed filling, and the objects needed securing. Lyra steeled herself and continued working, though her skin prickled each time she handled certain items. A compass whose needle pointed toward her rather than north. A small wooden box that hummed when opened. A chess piece that felt warm despite the cool air.

They had nearly finished when Lyra’s attention was drawn to a simple wooden stand in the farthest corner. Unlike the elaborate displays elsewhere, this stand supported only a plain scabbarded sword. Its hilt was unadorned metal, its scabbard weathered leather without decoration. Among the gleaming treasures, it seemed almost intentionally unremarkable.

Yet she couldn’t look away.

“Lyra?” Marla’s voice sounded distant. “We need to finish before the guard returns.”

“Yes,” she responded automatically, still staring at the sword. Something about it called to her—not a sound, but a sensation similar to the pressure behind her ears before a storm breaks.

“We should pack that last,” Marla said, following her gaze. “Looks heavy.”

Lyra nodded and turned back to the task at hand, but her attention repeatedly drifted to the sword. As they secured the remaining treasures, the ship’s movement grew more pronounced. The approaching storm had arrived, sending the Tempest lurching through increasingly hostile waters.

A particularly violent wave caught them unprepared. The ship heaved, and Lyra stumbled against a crate. Various objects they hadn’t yet secured slid across shelves. The sword—still untouched on its stand—trembled but remained upright.

“We need to hurry,” Marla urged, steadying herself against a column.

They worked faster, moving the remaining items to their designated crates. All except the sword, which they’d left for last. Another wave struck, stronger than the last. The ship groaned around them, timbers protesting the sea’s assault. This time, the sword tilted on its stand.

“I’ll get it,” Lyra said, crossing to the corner before Marla could object.

As her hands closed around the scabbard, a jolt passed through her entire body—like touching metal during a lightning storm, but from the inside out. She gasped, nearly dropping the sword, but her fingers seemed unwilling to release it.

“Lyra?” Marla’s concerned voice barely registered.

The sword was heavier than its size suggested, requiring both hands to lift properly. Despite its plain appearance, the balance was perfect, the weight distributed with precision that spoke of master craftsmanship. As Lyra carried it toward its designated crate, she found herself wondering what it might look like unsheathed.

The thought had barely formed when another massive wave struck. The Tempest pitched violently, sending both women stumbling. Lyra hit the floor hard, the sword flying from her grasp. She threw out her hands to break her fall, feeling the bite of splinters as her palms scraped across rough planking.

The ship stabilized momentarily. Lyra pushed herself up, wincing at the sting in her hands. Small bits of wood had embedded themselves in her right palm, drawing pinpricks of blood.

“Are you hurt?” Marla asked, helping her to her feet.

“Just splinters,” Lyra assured her, though the pain was sharp. She searched for the sword and spotted it a few feet away, its scabbard partially dislodged by the impact.

“Leave it,” Marla advised. “Your hand needs cleaning.”

But some compulsion drove Lyra forward. “We need to secure everything,” she insisted, reaching for the weapon.

Her bloodied palm touched the exposed blade.

The world stopped.

Later, Lyra would struggle to describe what happened next. It wasn’t pain, exactly, though her body arched with the shock of it. It wasn’t sound, though something roared through her consciousness. It was as if someone had drawn a line down the center of reality and folded it in half, connecting points never meant to touch.

Her vision tunneled to blackness, then exploded into red-tinged fragments. Through them, impressions flashed with brutal clarity—a forge glowing with unnatural fire; a mountain splitting open to reveal crystalline depths; figures in robes chanting words that burned the air; a battlefield strewn with bodies, the ground drinking blood like water. Centuries compressed into seconds, washing over her in waves of sensation too intense to process.

Then, cutting through it all, a voice.

«Finally.»

The word formed directly in her mind, bypassing her ears entirely. It carried texture—rough, metallic, ancient—and overwhelming relief, like a drowning man’s first breath after breaking the surface.

The pressure increased, expanding inside her skull until she was certain it would shatter. Fragmented thoughts that weren’t hers invaded her consciousness: «—after so long—» «—weak vessel but potential—» «—blood connection established—»

“Lyra!” Marla’s panicked cry seemed to come from miles away.

With tremendous effort, Lyra dragged her hand away from the blade. The connection thinned but didn’t break. Whatever had entered her mind remained, its presence a weight behind her eyes, observing through them.

«Your name is Lyra.» Not a question. The voice was clearer now, distinctly masculine but with an odd harmonic quality, as if multiple speakers perfectly synchronized.

She stumbled backward, clutching her hand to her chest. The small blood smear on the blade gleamed with unnatural brightness.

“Lyra, what’s wrong?” Marla gripped her shoulders, face tight with concern. “You screamed and then just… froze.”

Had she screamed? She couldn’t remember. Her throat felt raw enough.

“I—” Her voice failed. How could she explain what was happening when she didn’t understand it herself? “Something’s wrong with that sword.”

«Nothing is wrong with me, girl. I am exactly as I was forged to be.» The voice again, edged with amusement now. «You, however, are unexpectedly fragile.»

Lyra’s knees buckled. Marla caught her, barely managing to guide her to the floor instead of letting her collapse. The older woman’s eyes darted between Lyra’s face and the sword, connecting them even without understanding how.

“It’s speaking to me,” Lyra whispered, knowing how mad she sounded.

Marla’s expression shifted from concern to fear. She made a quick gesture Lyra recognized—a village ward against evil. “That’s not possible. Unless…” Her voice dropped lower. “Is it magic? Like the artifacts Blackwood seeks?”

«Tell the crone I am far more than a mere artifact. I am Chaos embodied, Order’s necessary counterweight, forged in fires that would consume her pitiful village in heartbeats.»

“It can hear you,” Lyra said, pressing her palms against her temples. “It’s… angry. Proud.”

The ship lurched again, reminding them of the storm still raging outside. Marla glanced anxiously at the door. “The guard will return soon. We need to secure everything, including… that.”

Lyra nodded, though movement made the presence in her mind shift uncomfortably. She pushed herself to her feet and approached the sword again, fighting her body’s instinct to flee.

«You cannot flee from me, little fish. We are bound now, you and I.»

“What do you mean, bound?” she asked aloud, forgetting Marla for a moment.

«Blood to metal. Life to purpose. The oldest magic, irreversible as death.»

Cold terror washed through her. “No. I didn’t agree to anything.”

«Agreement is irrelevant. The bond forms or it doesn’t. With you, it formed instantly.» A pause, during which Lyra felt something like surprised assessment. «Interesting. You have potential I didn’t initially sense.»

Marla watched this one-sided conversation with growing alarm. “Lyra, whatever it’s saying, don’t listen. We need to put it away and tell the guard you’ve taken ill.”

But Lyra knew, with devastating certainty, that containing this situation wouldn’t be so simple. The voice had embedded itself in her consciousness like a fishhook—attempting to remove it would only cause more damage.

With trembling hands, she picked up the sword and slid it fully back into its scabbard. The mental presence dimmed slightly but remained, watching from behind her eyes.

«A temporary measure,» the voice commented. «The physical barrier changes nothing about our connection.»

Together, she and Marla secured the sword in its designated crate, working quickly as the storm continued to punish the ship. Every movement felt strange to Lyra now, as if her body were a poorly fitting garment. The presence observed her actions with detached curiosity.

They had just finished when the door unlocked, revealing their guard. His eyes narrowed at Lyra’s pallor and Marla’s obvious distress.

“Everything secured?” he demanded.

Marla nodded quickly. “Yes. All items are in their crates as ordered.”

The guard surveyed the room, then jerked his head toward the corridor. “Back to the hold with you, then.”

As they followed him through the heaving ship, Lyra struggled to control her mounting panic. The voice maintained a running commentary on their surroundings, noting structural weaknesses in the vessel, categorizing the weapons carried by passing crew members, assessing threats and escape routes with cold precision.

«This ship has taken at least fourteen lives under its current captain. I can taste the death in the timbers.»

“Stop,” Lyra whispered, earning a suspicious glance from the guard.

«You’ll need to learn to speak to me silently, girl, unless you wish to appear mad. Simply direct your thoughts. I will hear them.»

Leave me alone, she tried, focusing the thought into a directed stream.

«Impossible. We are bound until death—yours or mine.» A pause. «And I do not die easily.»

The implications of that statement washed over her as they descended toward the hold. Whatever had happened in that treasure room had fundamentally altered her existence. She wasn’t just a captive of Blackwood now, but of something far older and more dangerous.

The hatch to the hold opened, and the guard gestured them inside. As Lyra descended the ladder, the voice offered one final observation.

«Your fellow prisoners are weak. Half will die before this journey ends. But you, little fish… you will survive. I require it.»

The hatch slammed shut above them, plunging them back into dimness. Familiar smells and sounds enveloped Lyra—unwashed bodies, quiet weeping, the creak of the ship. But nothing felt familiar anymore, not even her own mind.

Farro shifted to make space for her in their corner. “You look like you’ve seen a ghost, girl.”

“Worse,” she murmured, drawing her knees to her chest. “I think I’ve become one.”

The sword’s amusement rippled through her consciousness. «Not a ghost. A vessel. My vessel. And together, we will reshape this pitiful world according to our will.»

Lyra closed her eyes, but darkness offered no escape from the voice that had taken residence within her. The storm raged outside, waves battering the ship from all directions. But the true tempest had taken root inside her, and there was no shore in sight.

Chapter 4: Unwelcome Voices

Sleep had become a luxury Lyra could no longer afford.

Three days had passed since her blood had touched the blade—three days of constant, unwanted company inside her own mind. Even in the rare moments when unconsciousness claimed her, the sword pushed images through her dreams: battlefields strewn with corpses, ancient rituals performed in forgotten temples, faces of past wielders contorted in expressions of power and madness.

She woke gasping from one such vision to find the hold in pre-dawn darkness. Around her, other captives slept in uncomfortable positions, their breathing creating a chorus of soft rasps and occasional whimpers. No one had noticed her distress. No one except the presence that never left.

«Weakness,» the sword commented. «Even your nightmares lack proper violence.»

Lyra pressed her palms against her temples, a futile gesture she couldn’t stop repeating despite knowing the voice came from within, not without.

Leave me alone, she thought fiercely, directing the words inward as she’d learned to do after too many suspicious glances from fellow prisoners who’d caught her speaking aloud to nothing.

«Impossible,» came the immediate reply, tinged with what might have been amusement. «I’ve waited centuries for a compatible vessel. Did you think I would simply remain silent now that I’ve found one?»

Lyra curled tighter against the damp wooden hull, trying to create some illusion of privacy in the crowded hold. The sword’s constant presence made even this small space feel suffocating, as if she were being watched while changing clothes or bathing.

«Your discomfort is irrelevant,» the sword continued. «What matters is how thoroughly unprepared you are for the potential I offer. Your combat instincts are nonexistent. Your magical awareness is negligible despite obvious latent ability. And your willingness to kill—» The mental equivalent of a disappointed sigh filtered through her consciousness. «—is embarrassingly absent.»

I’m not a killer, Lyra responded, the words forming more clearly now with practice. I never wanted to be.

«What you want is inconsequential. What you are becoming is inevitable.»

A sound from across the hold interrupted their exchange—the hatch opening to admit weak gray light. A guard’s silhouette appeared against the square of sky.

“Up! Captain’s orders—all of you topside!” The command was punctuated by a wooden club striking against the hatch frame.

Around Lyra, captives stirred with varying degrees of reluctance. Some had adapted to the routine of captivity with alarming speed, while others still moved as if in a daze. Marla, who had witnessed Lyra’s bonding with the sword, kept her distance now, eyeing her with a mixture of fear and pity.

«This could be an opportunity,» the sword observed as prisoners began climbing the ladder toward daylight. «Strike when they least expect it. I can guide your movements. The guard at the top has a knife poorly secured at his belt.»

Shut up, Lyra thought with unexpected ferocity. I’m not attacking anyone. There are children here who would be punished for my actions.

The sword’s displeasure manifested as a sharp pain behind her eyes. «Your compassion for these weaklings is misplaced. They are already doomed.»

Lyra ignored this as she followed the others up the ladder, squinting as full daylight assaulted her vision. The day was clear, the sky a pristine blue that seemed to mock their captivity. The Tempest cut through calm waters, part of a fleet whose other vessels dotted the horizon. Crew members moved purposefully around them, barely sparing glances for the captives now assembled on the main deck.

«Six immediate threats within striking distance,» the sword assessed. «The heavyset man to your right is favoring his left leg. Begin with him.»

I said no! Lyra mentally shouted, earning a curious glance from the young woman beside her—a slim, sharp-featured girl perhaps a year or two younger than herself, with a distinctive white streak running through her dark hair.

Before she could look away, the girl spoke in a barely audible whisper. “You’re talking to yourself again.”

Lyra froze, her heart hammering against her ribs. Had she spoken aloud without realizing it?

“I don’t know what you mean,” she murmured, keeping her eyes fixed on the planks beneath her feet.

The girl’s response was interrupted by the appearance of the officer with the twisted mouth—the same woman who had selected Lyra for treasure-securing duty during the storm. She surveyed the assembled captives with cold efficiency.

“Captain Blackwood has decided you’ll earn your keep while aboard,” she announced. “You’ll be assigned duties according to your capabilities. Those who work well may earn better conditions. Those who resist…” She let the threat hang unfinished in the salt-laden air.

The captives were divided into groups—some sent to the galley to assist the cook, others assigned to cleaning duties, the strongest directed to help with physical labor. Lyra found herself among those selected for laundry duty, along with the sharp-featured girl and three others.

They were led to a section of the lower deck where large wooden tubs waited, already filled with seawater. Bundles of soiled clothing—shirts, trousers, and coats belonging to the crew—were stacked nearby. Their guard, a wiry man with a pockmarked face, gave terse instructions before leaving them to the task, locking the door behind him.

The work was grueling. The salt water stung the cuts and scrapes on Lyra’s hands, and the constant bending and scrubbing soon made her back ache. But the physical discomfort provided a welcome distraction from the sword’s unrelenting commentary.

«Menial labor,» it sneered. «I was forged to sever corrupted magical connections and defend realm boundaries, not to wash pirates’ smallclothes.»

The absurdity of the situation—a supposedly ancient magical artifact complaining about laundry—nearly startled a laugh from Lyra’s throat. She caught herself just in time, biting her lip as she plunged another shirt into the tub.

“It gets easier if you stop fighting it,” the sharp-featured girl said quietly beside her, wringing water from a pair of trousers.

Lyra’s hands stilled. “What?”

The girl glanced around to ensure the others were occupied with their own work before continuing. “The voice. The one you’re arguing with when you think no one’s watching.”

Cold fear washed through Lyra. “I don’t know what—”

“Save it.” The girl cut her off with surprising authority. “I’ve seen this before. You’ve bonded with something, haven’t you? Something magical.”

«She knows too much,» the sword hissed. «Silence her before she exposes our connection.»

Lyra ignored the sword, studying the girl’s face for signs of deception or threat. She found neither, only a frank curiosity tinged with caution.

“Who are you?” Lyra asked finally.

“Nari. Formerly of the Seaflower, currently of nowhere desirable.” She wrung another garment with more force than necessary. “My father was Captain Kerrigan. Blackwood killed him three years ago and kept me as leverage against his former allies.”

The name meant nothing to Lyra, but the sword stirred with recognition.

«Kerrigan of the Seaflower,» it mused. «Known for precision raids on merchant vessels. Preferred speed and strategy over brute force. Not without skill.»

“I’m Lyra,” she offered in return. “From Stillwater village.” The words felt hollow, the place they described no longer existing except in memory.

Nari nodded. “I know. I’ve been aboard longer than most. I notice things.” Her eyes flicked meaningfully to Lyra’s midsection, where the sword would be if it were physically present. “Like how you were normal when they first brought you aboard, and now you’re having conversations with empty air.”

Lyra’s instinct was to deny everything, but exhaustion and the desperate need for any ally overwhelmed caution.

“It’s a sword,” she admitted in a whisper. “In the treasure room. During the storm. I cut my hand, and it… connected to me somehow.”

Nari sucked in a sharp breath. “One of the artifacts. Gods below.” She leaned closer. “Listen carefully. Don’t let Blackwood know. She collects them—magical items. It’s become an obsession. If she realizes you’ve bonded with one…”

“Too late for that warning,” a melodic voice interrupted from the doorway.

Both women startled, dropping the laundry back into the tubs. Captain Blackwood stood in the entrance, her tall figure silhouetted against the corridor’s lamplight. Behind her loomed two guards, their hands resting on weapons.

«Finally,» the sword purred in Lyra’s mind. «The one truly worth killing on this floating prison.»

Blackwood stepped into the room, her movements fluid and controlled. The other prisoners shrank back, pressing themselves against the far wall. Only Nari stood her ground beside Lyra, though her face had gone pale.

“You,” Blackwood addressed Lyra directly. “You’ve been working in my treasure room.”

It wasn’t a question, but Lyra nodded anyway, her mouth suddenly dry.

“And you found something that spoke to you. That called to your blood.” The captain’s gaze was unnervingly intense. “Something that now whispers constantly in your mind, offering power in exchange for obedience.”

Lyra couldn’t suppress a small gasp. The accuracy of the description sent a chill down her spine.

«She knows of our kind,» the sword observed. «She has encountered others like me.»

Blackwood smiled thinly, recognizing Lyra’s reaction as confirmation. “Bring her,” she ordered the guards. “The rest continue working.”

“Captain—” Nari began, taking a half-step forward.

“Stay in your place, Kerrigan’s daughter,” Blackwood cut her off without looking away from Lyra. “Unless you prefer less comfortable accommodations.”

The threat silenced Nari, though her eyes communicated a silent warning to Lyra as the guards moved forward to seize her arms.

“No need for that,” Lyra said, finding her voice at last. “I’ll come willingly.”

«Cooperate for now,» the sword advised, its tone calculating. «Learn what she knows of artifacts. Then we’ll plan her execution.»

The corridors of the Tempest became a maze as the guards led her deeper into the ship than she’d been before. They ascended a narrow staircase to what must have been the captain’s quarters—a surprisingly spacious cabin at the stern, with large windows overlooking the ship’s wake.

Unlike the utilitarian nature of the rest of the vessel, Blackwood’s private domain reflected wealth and calculated intimidation. Exotic weapons hung on the walls. A massive desk dominated the center, its surface covered with maps and nautical instruments. Glass cases displayed smaller treasures—jewels, figurines, and objects Lyra couldn’t identify but which radiated a subtle wrongness that made her skin prickle.

«Objects of power,» the sword identified. «Minor artifacts, most of them. Trinkets compared to what I offer.»

Blackwood dismissed the guards with a gesture, then circled her desk to stand before Lyra. Up close, the captain was even more imposing. Her scar, running from temple to jaw, pulled slightly at one corner of her mouth, giving the impression of a perpetual half-smile.

“So,” she said, studying Lyra as one might examine an unexpected but interesting insect. “The Sword of Chaos has chosen you. How… unexpected.”

The name sent a ripple of satisfaction through the sword’s consciousness. «She knows me by reputation. Good.»

“I didn’t choose anything,” Lyra replied, struggling to keep her voice steady. “It forced itself on me when my blood touched the blade.”

Blackwood laughed, the sound genuinely amused. “That’s how they work, girl. Blood and trauma forge the strongest bonds.” She turned away, moving to a cabinet inlaid with mother-of-pearl. From it, she withdrew a decanter and poured amber liquid into two glasses. “Drink,” she commanded, offering one to Lyra.

«Poison is a possibility,» the sword warned. «Though she seems more interested in us alive than dead.»

Lyra accepted the glass but didn’t drink. “What do you want from me?”

“Direct. I appreciate that.” Blackwood sipped from her own glass. “What I want is simple. I want the Sword of Chaos. I’ve spent years searching for the Four Artifacts of Balance. I already possess two.”

With calculated casualness, she gestured toward a seemingly empty corner of the cabin. For a moment, Lyra saw nothing. Then, as if responding to its mistress’s attention, a silver chalice materialized on a pedestal, its surface rippling like disturbed water despite being solid metal.

«The Chalice of Tides,» the sword identified, an unexpected note of recognition in its mental voice. «My sibling of water and order. It has been… centuries.»

Blackwood noted Lyra’s reaction. “Ah, your blade recognizes its old companion. And here—” She indicated an ordinary-looking quill pen resting on her desk. “The Feather of Whispers.”

As if hearing its name, the quill twitched slightly, leaving a faint golden trail in the air.

«Another sibling,» the sword acknowledged. «Air and knowledge. Three of us, gathered after so long apart.»

“You understand now,” Blackwood continued, circling Lyra like a predator. “I am close to reuniting all four artifacts. Only the Root of Stone eludes me still, and I have promising leads.” She stopped directly behind Lyra. “But there’s a complication.”

The captain’s breath was warm against Lyra’s ear, making her shiver involuntarily.

“Once bonded to a wielder, artifacts cannot simply be taken. They must be relinquished willingly, or…” She let the implication hang in the air.

«Or the wielder must die,» the sword completed silently. «Though death doesn’t always break the stronger bonds.»

“You want me to give you the sword,” Lyra said flatly.

Blackwood moved back into view, her expression calculating. “Eventually. For now, I want to understand this unexpected development. The sword is notoriously… particular about its wielders. It has rejected or destroyed dozens of potential candidates I’ve exposed to it.”

“Lucky me,” Lyra muttered.

The captain smiled, cold and sharp. “Indeed. The question is why. What makes you—a simple village girl with no apparent magical training—suitable for one of the most powerful artifacts ever created?” She reached out suddenly, grasping Lyra’s chin and tilting her face to examine it more closely. “There must be something I’m missing.”

The sword’s rage flared at the contact, sending a spike of pain through Lyra’s temple. «Strike her. Now. My power flows through your blood. Use it!»

The command was so forceful that Lyra’s hand actually twitched upward before she regained control. Blackwood noticed the movement and released her, stepping back with newfound wariness.

“Interesting,” she murmured. “It protects you. Or perhaps it simply doesn’t like others touching its property.”

“I’m not property,” Lyra snapped, the words emerging before she could consider their wisdom.

Blackwood laughed again. “Tell that to the blade whispering in your thoughts. It’s been dominating wielders for centuries.” She returned to her desk, setting down her glass. “But I’m getting ahead of myself. You need proper context to understand the position you’re in.”

She withdrew a rolled parchment from a drawer and spread it on the desk’s surface. Lyra recognized it immediately—the same map Blackwood had shown Merina before killing her. The memory sent a fresh wave of grief and anger through her.

«Use that anger,» the sword urged. «Channel it. Let me show you how.»

“The Four Artifacts of Balance,” Blackwood explained, gesturing to symbols marked at different points on the map. “Created during the Era of Four Realms to maintain equilibrium between elemental forces. Each represents a fundamental magical principle—chaos, order, knowledge, and stability.” She tapped each symbol in turn. “They were separated after the Great Collapse when their combined power proved too dangerous to contain in one location.”

Despite herself, Lyra found her attention caught by the history. “Why are you collecting them?”

“Power,” Blackwood stated simply. “Not just physical power, though that’s considerable, but the power to reshape the very nature of reality.” Her eyes gleamed with fervor that bordered on madness. “Imagine controlling the fundamental forces that govern existence. No kingdom could stand against such might. No law would apply. Absolute freedom.”

«Her ambition exceeds her understanding,» the sword commented. «The artifacts were never meant for conquest. Such use led to the corruption that now plagues us all.»

This information surprised Lyra. You were meant for something else? she directed the thought inward.

«A discussion for another time,» the sword deflected. «When we’re not in the presence of one who would misuse us further.»

Blackwood was watching Lyra’s face closely. “You’re communicating with it now, aren’t you? I can see the subtle changes in your expression.” She leaned forward, suddenly intense. “What does it tell you about the artifacts’ true purpose?”

“Nothing I’m willing to share with you,” Lyra replied, surprised by her own boldness.

The captain’s expression hardened. “I’ve been searching for these artifacts for five years. I’ve sacrificed more than you can imagine to collect them. If you think your temporary bond gives you any leverage here, you’re sorely mistaken.”

She straightened, composure returning like a mask sliding back into place. “But I’m a practical woman. Fighting your bond would be counterproductive for now. Instead, we’ll work with it.”

“What does that mean?” Lyra asked, suspicion sharpening her tone.

“It means,” Blackwood replied with calculating precision, “that starting tomorrow, you’ll be trained in combat. The sword chose you for a reason. Let’s discover what that reason is.”

«Finally,» the sword’s satisfaction pulsed through their connection. «A chance to develop your potential.»

Don’t sound so pleased, Lyra shot back. She still plans to separate us eventually.

«She can try,» the sword responded with cold certainty. «Many have. All have failed.»

“Return her to the general quarters,” Blackwood instructed as she opened the cabin door, revealing the waiting guards. “Ensure she’s separated from the Kerrigan girl. I don’t want them sharing information.”

As the guards led Lyra back through the ship’s corridors, her mind raced with implications. Blackwood knew far more about the sword than she did—knowledge that might help Lyra understand and potentially control this unwanted bond. But the captain’s ultimate goal remained their separation, which the sword seemed confident would prove fatal.

“In here,” one guard grunted, opening the door to a small cabin rather than the hold where the other villagers were kept. “Captain’s orders.”

The space was tiny—barely room for a narrow bunk and small chest—but infinitely better than the crowded prisoner hold. Lyra stepped inside, momentarily confused by this apparent preferential treatment.

“Don’t get comfortable,” the guard advised, his hand lingering on the door. “Training starts at dawn. And it won’t be gentle.”

The door closed, followed by the definitive sound of a lock engaging.

«Isolation,» the sword observed. «A standard tactic when dealing with new wielders. Remove support systems, increase dependency on the artifact.»

Lyra sank onto the bunk, suddenly aware of the bone-deep exhaustion she’d been fighting. The thin mattress felt luxurious after days of sleeping on bare planks.

«Rest while you can,» the sword advised, its voice taking on an almost tutoring tone. «Tomorrow begins your true education.»

I never asked for this, Lyra thought, closing her eyes against the reality of her situation.

«Few worthy wielders do. Power seeks those who wouldn’t abuse it, then ironic ally corrupts them through necessity.» The sword’s mental voice carried a weight of experiences Lyra couldn’t begin to comprehend. «Sleep. You’ll need your strength.»

For once, Lyra didn’t argue. Exhaustion claimed her almost immediately, dragging her into dreams filled with battles she hadn’t fought and faces she’d never seen—the sword’s memories bleeding into her own, erasing the boundaries between them one night at a time.

Dawn arrived with a pounding on the cabin door that jolted Lyra from uneasy sleep. The sword had been active in her dreams again, showing her combat techniques performed by previous wielders, their movements fluid and lethal.

“Up!” a voice commanded. “On deck in five minutes.”

She rose, feeling the ship’s gentle rocking beneath her feet. A pitcher of water and clean clothing had been left sometime during the night—simple trousers and a shirt similar to what the crew wore, rather than her increasingly ragged village dress.

«They’re preparing you for your role,» the sword noted. «Creating distance between you and your former identity.»

Lyra changed quickly, uncomfortably aware of the sword’s presence even in this private moment. The new clothes fit reasonably well, though they carried the scent of previous owners and harsh soap. She splashed water on her face, trying to clear the fog of disturbed sleep.

The main deck was awash in early morning light when she emerged. Most of the crew were already engaged in their duties, paying her little attention. Near the foremast, a cleared space had been created, with various weapons laid out on a canvas sheet.

Captain Blackwood waited there, no longer wearing her elaborate coat but instead dressed in simple, practical clothing that emphasized her lean, muscular build. Beside her stood a tall man with dark skin and a shaved head, his arms crossed over a broad chest.

«Interesting,» the sword commented. «The captain trains you personally rather than delegating. She’s more intrigued by our bond than she admitted.»

Lyra approached cautiously, acutely aware of curious glances from nearby crew members. Whatever was about to happen, it would have an audience.

“You slept well?” Blackwood asked, though her tone suggested she cared little for the answer.

“Well enough,” Lyra replied neutrally.

The captain nodded toward the weapons laid out on the canvas. “Choose one.”

Lyra stared at the array—knives of various lengths, a short sword, a wooden staff, even what appeared to be a small axe. None called to her; they were just objects, lacking the unsettling sentience of the artifact bound to her.

«The short sword,» the blade advised in her mind. «Similar weight and balance to my physical form. It will prepare your muscles properly.»

Without questioning why she followed its counsel, Lyra reached for the indicated weapon. It felt awkward in her hand, nothing like the perfect balance she’d briefly experienced with the actual sword.

“Interesting choice,” Blackwood observed. “Let’s see what instincts the artifact has given you.” She nodded to the man beside her. “Koric will test your defenses. Try not to get killed.”

Before Lyra could process this alarming instruction, the man—Koric—moved toward her with predatory grace, a wooden practice sword in his hand. His first strike was relatively slow, clearly intended to gauge her response rather than cause harm.

Lyra’s body reacted before her mind fully registered the attack. She stepped sideways, bringing her borrowed sword up in a defensive motion she had never practiced but somehow knew. The blades connected with a solid thwack that reverberated up her arm.

«Better than expected,» the sword acknowledged, «but still pathetic. Your grip is wrong. Feet shoulder-width apart. Weight on the balls of your feet, not the heels.»

The next strike came faster. Lyra managed to block again, but barely, the force driving her back a step.

«You’re defending when you should be attacking!» the sword raged. «Strike at his exposed side!»

But Lyra couldn’t reconcile the idea of deliberately attacking another person, even in training. She maintained her defensive posture, blocking as best she could while Koric increased the speed and complexity of his attacks.

Within minutes, sweat poured down her face. Her arms trembled with effort. A particularly forceful blow knocked the practice sword from her grip, sending it clattering across the deck.

“Enough,” Blackwood called, stepping forward. “Your technique is atrocious, but you shouldn’t have any instinct for swordplay at all.” Her eyes narrowed. “The artifact is helping you, isn’t it? Guiding your movements?”

Lyra nodded, too winded to speak. In truth, the sword had been shouting instructions constantly, growing increasingly frustrated when she failed to follow its more aggressive suggestions.

«You fight its guidance,» Blackwood observed, her perception uncomfortably accurate. “That’s why your movements are disjointed. You’re caught between its combat knowledge and your own reluctance.”

«The pirate understands better than you do,» the sword hissed. «Stop resisting and let me make you formidable.»

I don’t want to be formidable, Lyra thought back. I just want to survive.

«Those are increasingly the same goal,» the sword replied ominously.

Blackwood signaled to a nearby crew member. “Bring the artifact.”

Lyra’s pulse quickened. Despite the sword’s constant mental presence, she hadn’t seen its physical form since that moment in the treasure room when blood had sealed their bond.

The crew member returned carrying a wooden box. Blackwood opened it to reveal the Sword of Chaos, still in its plain leather scabbard, looking deceptively ordinary against the velvet lining.

«At last,» the sword’s anticipation surged through their connection. «Reunion with my physical form.»

As the box was brought closer, Lyra felt a curious sensation—like a phantom pulse beating in synchronization with her own heart. The sword’s mental presence grew stronger, more defined, its satisfaction radiating through their bond.

“Take it,” Blackwood commanded.

Lyra hesitated, fear warring with a strange, unwelcome longing. The sword’s physical form shouldn’t matter when its consciousness was already within her mind, yet she found herself drawn to it nonetheless.

«Embrace what you cannot escape,» the sword urged. «Take up your birthright.»

It’s not my birthright, she objected, even as her hand moved forward of its own accord.

Her fingers closed around the hilt. Immediately, warmth spread up her arm and across her chest, as if she’d stepped into sunlight after hours in shadow. The sword’s presence in her mind clarified, becoming less an invasive voice and more an extension of her own consciousness.

“Now,” Blackwood said, stepping back. “Try again.”

Koric moved into position, his expression professionally blank despite the unusual situation. He raised his practice sword in silent challenge.

Lyra drew the blade from its scabbard.

In the direct sunlight, it wasn’t as plain as she’d first thought. Subtle patterns ran along the metal, reminiscent of flames when caught at certain angles. The blade hummed almost imperceptibly, the vibration traveling up her arm and resonating with something deep within her.

«Together now,» the sword whispered, no longer harsh but seductive in its confidence. «Let me show you what we can accomplish.»

This time, when Koric attacked, Lyra moved with fluid precision. The sword seemed to anticipate each strike, guiding her body with subtle physical cues rather than verbal instructions. Block, parry, sidestep—the sequence emerged naturally, as if she’d practiced it for years.

Blackwood watched with narrowed eyes, her hand resting casually on the hilt of her own weapon. Around them, crew members had paused in their duties to observe the display, murmuring among themselves.

«Now, strike!» the sword commanded as Koric momentarily overextended. «Disable him permanently.»

The bloodthirsty intent behind the command shocked Lyra back to full awareness. Her body, which had been moving in perfect harmony with the blade’s guidance, suddenly faltered.

No! she rejected the command with every fiber of her being. This is training, not combat!

The sword’s rage crashed through their connection like a physical blow. «Weakness! Always weakness with you humans!»

Pain exploded behind Lyra’s eyes as the sword attempted to force compliance. Her vision blurred, her grip on the hilt loosening. She staggered back, away from Koric, away from the urge to maim that wasn’t hers but somehow resided within her.

“Stop,” she gasped, unsure whether she was addressing Koric or the sword itself.

Blackwood raised her hand, and Koric immediately lowered his weapon. The captain’s expression held genuine curiosity now—and something else. Recognition.

“You’re fighting it,” she observed. “Interesting. Most new wielders surrender to the power almost immediately.”

Lyra stood trembling, the sword still clutched in her hand but held away from her body as if it might bite. In her mind, the blade’s fury continued to batter against her resistance.

«You embarrass us both,» it seethed. «That opponent deserved incapacitation at minimum.»

He’s not an enemy, Lyra insisted. This is practice, and I won’t hurt someone unnecessarily.

“The conflict is written all over your face,” Blackwood commented, circling Lyra with clinical interest. “The Sword of Chaos has always been the most aggressive of the artifacts, demanding violence from its wielders as proof of worthiness.”

She stopped directly before Lyra, her scarred face impossible to read. “What does it want you to do right now?”

Lyra swallowed hard. “Kill. Always kill.”

Blackwood nodded as if this confirmed a theory. “And yet you resist. Most interesting.”

A new voice interrupted from nearby. “Captain, approaching vessel off the starboard bow. Merchant colors, but moving suspiciously fast.”

The distraction broke the tension of the moment. Blackwood turned away, immediately shifting into her role as commander. “Battle stations,” she ordered. “Prepare to intercept.”

The deck erupted into organized chaos as crew members rushed to their positions. Koric moved to follow, but Blackwood held him back with a gesture.

“Take her below,” she instructed. “Lock the artifact away, but not too far from her. I want to observe how distance affects their connection.”

Lyra felt a surge of panic at the thought of separation from the physical sword, even though its consciousness would remain with her. The blade evidently shared this concern, its anger transmuting to anxiety.

«Resist,» it commanded. «We must remain together.»

But Lyra was too exhausted from their mental battle to put up much physical resistance as Koric led her firmly toward the hatch. As they descended below decks, she heard the rising clamor of a ship preparing for combat—orders shouted, weapons distributed, strategies discussed.

“In here,” Koric directed, opening the door to what appeared to be an officer’s cabin, smaller than Blackwood’s but still private. “Stay until called for.”

Lyra entered without protest, but when he reached to take the sword from her hand, she instinctively tightened her grip.

«Do not relinquish me,» the sword demanded. «Fight!»

Koric’s expression hardened. “Don’t make this difficult, girl. Captain’s orders.”

They stood at impasse for a moment, tension building between them. Then, with deliberate calm, Lyra loosened her fingers from the hilt.

“Take it,” she said quietly, forcing the words past the sword’s howl of protest in her mind.

Koric accepted the blade carefully, his movements suggesting he understood its value if not its true nature. “The chest in the corner is prepared for it,” he explained, surprisingly patient despite the battle preparations continuing above. “Captain says it needs to be in your vicinity, but not in your possession. Testing your bond, though I don’t pretend to understand how.”

He placed the sword in an ornate box similar to the one it had been brought in, then closed the lid. Immediately, Lyra felt the connection thin—not breaking, but stretching like a thread pulled taut. The sword’s voice became fainter, though no less insistent.

«This separation is unnecessary torture,» it complained. «We function optimally in direct contact.»

Maybe I function better with some distance between us, Lyra thought, though without real conviction. Already she could feel a phantom ache developing, centered in her chest where no wound existed.

“You’ll remain here during the engagement,” Koric informed her, moving toward the door. “Food will be brought later. Don’t attempt to leave—the door will be locked, and the crew has orders.”

He paused at the threshold, studying her with an expression that might have been pity. “The captain has collected many strange things in her voyages, but you might be the strangest. For your sake, I hope the sword deems you worthy.” A shadow crossed his face. “I’ve seen what happens to those it rejects.”

Before Lyra could ask what he meant, the door closed and the lock engaged with a definitive click.

Left alone with the sword’s muted presence, Lyra moved to the small porthole. Outside, the Tempest was closing on what appeared to be a merchant vessel flying the colors of the Eastern Kingdoms. Even from this distance, she could see the panic on the other ship as it attempted to outrun its pursuer—a futile effort against Blackwood’s legendary vessel.

«You watch from a cage while blood is spilled without you,» the sword observed, its voice fainter but still clear enough. «This is not our purpose.»

Our purpose? Lyra questioned. You said before that you were created for something other than violence. What was it?

The sword’s presence hesitated, an unusual pause in its normally confident assertions. «Fragments remain. Memories buried beneath centuries of…use. I was forged to protect boundaries, to sever corrupted connections.»

Not to kill?

«Killing was incidental, not central.» Another pause. «This conversation depletes energy we cannot afford to waste while separated.»

Lyra sensed truth in this. Already the phantom ache in her chest had intensified, a persistent discomfort that made it difficult to focus on anything else. She moved to the bunk and sat, trying to regulate her breathing as the sounds of battle began above—shouts, running feet, the distant crack of what might have been cannon fire.

Minutes stretched into hours. The battle sounds gradually subsided, replaced by the organized chaos of aftermath—the cries of the wounded, commands being issued, the creak of ropes as captured cargo was transferred. Through it all, Lyra remained locked away, the sword’s presence a constant, if diminished, companion in her mind.

When the door finally opened, it was Nari who entered, carrying a tray of food. Her appearance surprised Lyra, who had expected a guard or perhaps Blackwood herself.

“You’re allowed to bring me food?” she asked as Nari set the tray on the small chest.

The girl glanced back at the open doorway where a guard stood watching. “I volunteered. Blackwood doesn’t care who delivers your meals as long as you’re monitored.” She lowered her voice. “Don’t react to what I’m about to say.”

Lyra maintained a neutral expression with effort, acutely aware of the guard’s eyes on them.

“Blackwood’s obsession with the artifacts is dangerous,” Nari murmured, arranging items on the tray with deliberate slowness. “They’re changing her. The crew’s noticed, though no one dares speak of it. Mood swings. Increasing recklessness. Talking to herself just like you do.”

«The artifacts corrupt those not strong enough to master them,» the sword commented. «Your Captain Blackwood handles power like a child with fire—fascinated by the flames while ignoring the spreading smoke.»

Lyra silently relayed this observation to Nari, who nodded almost imperceptibly.

“She’s planning something with you and that sword,” Nari continued, still pretending to arrange the food. “Something to do with the next artifact she’s hunting.”

“The Root of Stone,” Lyra said quietly.

Nari’s eyes widened slightly. “Yes. How did you—”

“She told me. She has the other two already.”

“Which is two too many,” Nari replied grimly. “One more minute,” she called to the guard before turning back to Lyra. “Listen, if you get a chance to escape, take it. Don’t wait for me or anyone else. The artifacts are poison.”

«Yet here you are, bound to one,» the sword interjected with something like amusement.

Not by choice, Lyra retorted.

«Few worthy vessels choose their burden.»

The guard cleared his throat impatiently, and Nari straightened. “Eat while it’s warm,” she said at normal volume. “Better than the slop they serve the regular prisoners.”

As she turned to leave, Lyra caught her wrist. “Be careful,” she whispered, surprising herself with the genuine concern she felt for this girl she barely knew.

Nari’s expression softened momentarily before her mask of casual indifference returned. “Always am,” she replied, then departed with the guard, leaving Lyra alone once more.

The food was indeed better than what she’d had in the hold—fresh bread, some kind of fish stew, even a small cup of watered wine. Lyra ate mechanically, her appetite diminished by the constant discomfort of separation from the sword’s physical form.

«Proximity matters,» the sword explained as if sensing her thoughts. «The blood bond connects us regardless of distance, but physical closeness strengthens our communication and abilities.»

Is that why you’re showing me how to fight? To make me a better weapon?
